First of all: I was back with the family in Lassing yesterday. I have assumed my responsibility in all five areas raised by me. First of all, the recovery of those missing – perhaps a few words later – where the procedure continues. Second point: the care of the family. Thirdly, the continuing employment of workers at work. Fourth point: securing the future of the valley. Fifth point: working on cleaning in the legal area, in the mountain law. At any point I could talk here for a long time, but let me present it as briefly as possible. Secondly, I do not see any reason to let myself into a war with a company, whatever the reputation, on account of this occasion as Minister of Economic Affairs. I don't have a war with Rio Tinto. I'm dealing with the Naintsch company. The company Naintsch is sometimes difficult enough. It has appealed against the recovery notice, we have decided immediately, and the recovery will continue. There are always minors, such as the non-payment of invoices. We are interconnected and have pre-financed so that companies do not go bankrupt and we have taken the necessary measures. Now the company pays back again – no matter how cheerful. I have ensured that, if these difficulties persist, I speak to the head of Rio Tinto. At the moment, there is no need for this. I do not want to start an official war against any company from this occasion, but on the spot everything must run as we have planned. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP.]] A second point. Long before the accusation of possession was put into space elsewhere, I announced to the International Expert Commission, which today has its constituent meeting. It was clear from the outset that not only in this traditional area can it be found that experts can work on both the issue of rescue-mountain assessment and the establishment of future recovery efforts as well as, finally, an accompanying determination of causality. The next accusation: ILO. – This accusation goes completely empty as far as I am concerned. The measures provided for in the ILO Agreement were partly implemented by my mountain authorities, as far as we considered necessary, even without ratification in Austria. Some of this has already been done, some of it is now also done in the mountain law, and I have, on the other hand, led to this agreement being finally taken seriously at tomorrow's Council of Ministers meeting, which I do not have any influence on. - So much for the second point. Third point: I would assume that all the other measures announced will be continued in accordance with their needs. Let me call these shortly – and this is still going on, Mr President. I repeat: We've found out what we've learned from Lassing, because something like this can repeat again and again. Lassing is also a pan-European experience. This was found internationally at the mining symposium two weeks ago in Leoben. Commissioner Padraig Flynn wrote me a letter yesterday, saying: "Aus Lassing must learn all Europe." This learning process has been initiated by us. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP.]] Other points: The establishment of a training course for crisis management has already begun. It will take place in Leoben in the first half of 1999. The construction of the mobile deployment force has already been commissioned and is in progress. Next point: We are already working in the house on the construction of the national computer file about auxiliary equipment and teams. It is in the meantime that we are going to launch such a file in Europe. We have also launched the digitisation of the mine plans. It should be continued as quickly as possible. With regard to the dissatisfaction with regard to marker separators, I should like to remind you that there is a legal system in this respect which has been decided to provide for transitional arrangements by the next summer. This can be speeded up. Next point: Structure of the mountain authorities. In the draft, which will be passed by the Council of Ministers tomorrow, we have provided for the resolution of the mountain authorities. There will only be one authority in the Ministry of Economic Affairs, which will then work according to another strategy and service concept. This new mountain law also takes all measures that take into account the justified expectations of residents, communities and countries. For the accusation: Brenner brickwork. I can only say that the causes of the slides at the Brenner brickworks are based on the geological structure of the slope and the local hydrogeological conditions. Due to the degradation of clay in the early 1960s, the labile slope was weakened and thus the terrain movements were favoured. At that time, the administrative permits and supervision of the mining were not with the mountain captains, but with the district authorities. [[Abg. Dr. Petrovic: It was legal with the mountain authorities! Since 1990!]] [[Abg. Wabl: Since 1990!]] Only by the mountain law novels... – let me speak out – only by the mountain law novel in 1990 did this area become a mining area. At that time, the buildings of the Clary estate had already been built, although the construction authority should have recognized first instance that it was a slip. [[Abg. Wabl: Oh, now the mayors are to blame! The Mountain Authority has nothing to do with this!]] As a result of the action of the Mountain Head Team Klagenfurt, the sliding slope below the property Clary was once again stabilized so that no ground movements can be detected. The claims made in this context in the media that the buildings are at acute risk of collapse are irrelevant after expert opinion. This has also been confirmed by the construction expert. Inaccurate is the fact that three estates are affected by the slide. The damage to the other buildings cannot be traced back to the slide. – So much about the findings in this regard. One further comment, because this has been claimed recently in the area, namely the question of whether our internal regulations are actually at the last technical level of things; a reference also to various experts. Ladies and gentlemen! I am happy to discuss in detail the fact that we have tried to put the state of the art into practice in the relevant regulations in all the regulations adopted in the meantime since 1995. This includes the Mountain Police Regulation for Electrical Engineering from 1996, the 1995 electromagnetic impact assessment, the 1997 Mountain Police Regulation on Responsible Persons and the 1997 Markscheider Regulation with transitional arrangements until this year. ParlaMint-AT_1998-10-07-020-XX-NRSITZ-00141_d7e5259	Mr President. Mrs. Federal Minister! Mr. Federal Minister! High house! The agenda items that have been summarized are comprehensive, so I would like to focus on the Atomic Liability Act. My colleague, Mr Barmüller, will comment on other aspects that are being discussed today. In my opinion, however, we should use this debate as an opportunity to increase the impact of the anti-atomic policy rather than to divide us on this issue. That is why I would prefer a five-party resolution request rather than a four-party resolution request. To the Atomic Liability Act: I believe that this proposal will make a really significant progress and the Liberal Group welcomes the fact that, at the initiative of my colleague Mr Barmüller, it has already made a comprehensive request for this in the autumn of 1997. We are very pleased that some of the considerations that have been made in this proposal are also reflected in the government template which is now being adopted, in particular the paradigm change in the area of liability, which is under the heading "channelisation" or "absorbing this canalization". This decision, which is likely to take place today, should be taken to refer to the paradigm change in continental European nuclear liability rights, while at the same time inviting the Federal Government to make this principle, which we have now developed, every opportunity to discuss with the Members of the European Union. The departure from the principle of canalization is a quantum leap in the pursuit of cost-truth in the production of electricity by nuclear splitting. In the United States, due to the different legal architecture, we were told that, if liability issues have to be taken seriously by operators, investments in such plants, such as nuclear power plants, will no longer be made at all. In my opinion, this method of enabling, speeding up and implementing an exit should not be disputed with all people, even with those who may still be of the stubborn opinion, it is nevertheless an advanced and harmless technology. I consider this economic approach to the reduction of indirect subsidies and the cost-effectiveness of the abolition of the principle of channelling to be essential and at the heart of the new Atomic Liability Act. I am aware that we will not be able to obtain any general international popularity and that, in particular, the Federal Republic of Germany is facing a major test on this issue. Whether the canalization will be abolished in Germany in the short term, as far as possible tomorrow, is the real question of the Gretchen for the future government and not the question of the exit and its speed. The fact that it is necessary to get out of the way has generally spoken, even the majority of the population is now in favour. [[Abg. Wabl: The Freemans, too?]] Only, some thick people still believe that this can be extended. - The sentence: I'm in favour of pulling things into the length! ParlaMint-AT_1998-10-07-020-XX-NRSITZ-00141_d7e5356	Mr President. Ladies and gentlemen! High house! I can stress much of what my previous speaker, Mr Kier, said here. I do not wish to give an opinion on the statements or on the debate by Mr Schweitzer in so far as many of them are already speaking for themselves, such as the accusation that, at the time of the decision to withdraw from nuclear energy in Austria, nuclear power plants had been established in the context of Austria's nuclear power plants. If this is raised as an accusation to the government, then I am no longer quite sure whether one really wants to be taken seriously with such argumentation here. [[Abg. Böhacker: He just said what happened!]] I am not aware of the fact that a serious debate on the motion for a resolution, which concerns the Czech Republic, has not really taken place, because it is totally unrealistic to demand things in Cowboymanier, which everyone knows quite clearly about foreign policy that they are totally impossible. I therefore distance myself from dealing with it. [[Congratulations with the SPÖ and representatives of the ÖVP.]] In fact, I think it must be said that a great deal has been done recently, both within and for the European area. The Austrian Government's anti-atomic power plant policy has exerted significant influence within Europe with regard to the overall position on nuclear power, influence on questioning the situation, and ultimately also on the design within the programmes. I can point to that in the 5th century. The EU framework programme has achieved clear success by reducing the resources allocated to nuclear power plants for research, and by restructuring within this budget as a whole, to a greater extent than the expansion of nuclear power plants as a whole. The rest of these financial resources, which have been restricted, are now available to research and develop renewable energy forms. In my opinion, it is only through this that I believe that there has been a very significant change, a change in quality within Europe, which is also a success of the Austrian Government. Of course, you can ignore that, or you can express it self-confidently. I think we should do the second. One of the key points of a credible argument within Europe is, of course, the way in which one chooses in one's own field. There is something we have to discuss today and, I hope, will also decide – this is evidenced by the fact that we voted unanimously in committee. There is now the new liability right, the national liability law. We have deliberately decoupled ourselves from international development through the draft which is under discussion today. We have decoupled ourselves in so far as we also wanted to create direction-setting new regulations here. I can count it on my party that, under the leadership of the clubman Kostelka and the accession of a university professor from Graz, we have drawn up a draft that goes completely new ways – new paths which have often been called, but which are now quite slow to prove right, especially if one sets out in the international discussion demands that one has to submit with one's own credibility. The central provision of the new nuclear liability right is the unlimited, strict liability. This means that there is no liability limit in the liability right for installations up to now, as has been done and as is provided for in the International Liability Convention, and there is a strict liability, i.e. there is also liability in the event of force majeure. The justification for this is that the person who builds or causes such a source of danger will ultimately also have to deal with all the consequences that this source of danger may produce. This is, of course, also a damage within the plant in the event of force majeure, which subsequently causes damage to the environment. Another important step is that in the future it will also be possible, in the event of damage resulting from a nuclear power plant outside Austria, which will, however, be effective in Austria, to also claim compensation in Austria at the place where the damage actually occurred, according to the law of the place in which the damage was caused, according to Austrian law. Of course, the discussion has gone so far as to say: What can one really do with such a judgment? What can one do with a judgment that standardises claims for damages, for example in Slovakia, if this judgment may not be enforceable there? I accept that this objection is currently having its correctness and is challenging the issue. I also think, however, that on the other hand, one cannot overlook the fact that we have to look at the development in nuclear liability in the overall context of historical development and also of future development. In this spectrum, it is very easy to raise demands. The claim for a cross-border liability – after the claim for damages where the damage was incurred, and without forcing the injured, going abroad and suing the damage there – is simply a new quality of enforcement. I think this is a quality of enforcement that will enable us to enforce liability law in international discussions. We will be able to enforce it all the easier, and we will be able to demonstrate the possibility and the quality of this new system of liability as we can point out that we have already implemented it in our own country. Therefore, I believe that even if it can still be a long and difficult way to implement it, it is simply necessary to go in this direction. We have also removed the so-called canalization of liability – which Mr Kier has already suggested today. What that means is simple: Until now, the operator of the power plant had to be held liable, but not the supplier of components of the power plant. It may, of course, be the case that, in the end, a supplied component was the cause of a damage. Now it is absolutely impossible to see why, in the case where there is a power plant beyond the border and a supplier on this side of the border, I should not be able to contact the suppliers, just as the ordinary compensation regulations of the Civil Code provide for. It has so far been a derogation that should benefit nuclear power plant operators and nuclear power plant suppliers, especially the latter. The point was that one said in the origins: This is a new technology, which is particularly to be protected, and therefore the subcontractors should not be held liable. There is no longer any reason for such a scheme at all, and the opposite is the case: Such liability arrangements should also enable suppliers of components to know and calculate that they can be held liable at any time. I should like to point out – and this was also discussed in the Committee – what happens if delivery was not from Austria, but, for example, from Germany. In the pan-European area, there is a convention, a rule – the Lugano agreement – to enforce judgments that have been reached here. This means that a judgment of damages in Austria can also be enforced within Europe as a whole. It will be the responsibility of the Government to ensure that the Lugano Agreement not only joins the European countries but also the neighbouring countries, because we will be able to make it easier to implement what we are going to decide here today in these countries in the context of an execution procedure and because it will show that the way we have begun to take is the right one for the future. - Thank you.

ParlaMint-AT_1998-10-07-020-XX-NRSITZ-00141_d7e5671	Mr President. Mr. Minister! I would like to refer to item 7 of the agenda, namely the new nuclear liability law that Minister Bartenstein has just referred to. We continue with this law the independent nuclear policy, the independent Austrian path. This independent Austrian way closes us the pots for international compensation claims. That is, after the canalization has been mentioned: We do not use them, we do not sign these international agreements and therefore we cannot take part in the international pots in the event of damage. We have therefore taken this up knowingly, because these international agreements – there are several with the additional protocols – for radiation damage and the solutions for compensation from our point of view are not really efficient and these pots are not sufficiently taught. I would, however, like to make it clear that the emphasis is not yet sufficiently efficient and insufficient. If the international Community scheme is to be significantly improved, in particular with regard to a sufficient amount of coverage, then of course we need to debate it again in this House and consider what might be more useful to the Austrian injured: the independent Austrian path or the international solidarity community. The current law provides for an amount of unlimited liability for liability. In other words, it is by no means limited in the amount, but we have standardized an insurance scheme with a minimum amount of coverage for nuclear plant operators in the amount of 5.6 billion schilling. Assigned: In Austria, there will be no nuclear plant as a power plant, as a nuclear power plant, and foreign operators of power plants that may cause damage in our neighboring area, which will then lead to damage in Austria, we cannot, by this law, undertake to take out insurance in our sense. However, we have standardised that complaints against foreign operators can very well be filed in Austria and that foreign operators in Austria are sued under Austrian law – with Austrian courts. Subsidiary can also be used in the event of damage to suppliers, e.g. if companies located in Austria have supplied to nuclear power plants in our neighborhood and the damage cases have caused causally. Due to the particular danger of nuclear material, its preparation, transport and use for research and medical purposes, the liability has been highly normized – further than is provided for in the general ABGB provisions. We accept this for this particular area of danger. The nuclear danger and the liability it lays down justify this. However, it is primarily the ABGB. In addition to the principle of indebtedness and the principles of compensation of the ABGB, however, evidence is normized for those affected by radiation in the Atomic Liability Act, namely certain assumptions, so that the injured person can more easily represent the causality. We have also included environmental damage in the same way as in the Code of Liability. For precisely these two areas – the Code of Liability and the Atomic Liability Act – are not really projectable in their danger dimension from today's point of view. Therefore, in both cases it warrants its own liability legislation. Ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen! It remains to be hoped, however, that the case of urgency for this law does not occur, and there may be few to no application for this law.

ParlaMint-AT_1998-10-07-020-XX-NRSITZ-00141_d7e5964	Mr President. Ladies and gentlemen! High house! This nuclear liability law, which we now have to deal with, is, I think, a very "bright" signal to the other states, because it is very much based on the principle of the fundamental promotion of the nuclear economy, because it also, I think, provides a liability for higher violence and because it does not provide any limits of liability. Another "bright" signal is the departure from the canalization of this liability, and I think it is a milestone. Not only the operator of a nuclear plant can be used for replacement claims, but, if it appears more successful, also the assembly company or the company carrying out the transports. In principle – and this is also a significant progress in the enforcement of liability claims – an Austrian court can be called upon. Of course – and this is already true today – we are aware that the enforcement of an Austrian title to States with which there is no enforcement agreement is not guaranteed. We therefore strongly demand a cross-border liability, and the motion for a resolution also aims to ensure that the Federal Government takes action in this regard. It is very important for the practical enforcement of claims for damages by those concerned to be the established burden of proof. Thus, in the case of a human condition where it is likely that his body was exposed to ionising radiation from a nuclear plant or from nuclear material, the operation now has the proof that this radiation was not relevant to the disease, and not the ill if he wishes to assert his claims. – Austria is therefore taking a new path with this Atomic Liability Act, in principle with regard to the enforcement of claims for damages. A word to colleagues' head, which I no longer see in the hall. He has very vehemently demanded a nuclear ban law. [[Abg. Tichy-Schreder: He's sitting here! There he sits!]] That's from the old clothes collection. – For this, you have us social democrats right away. They have called for the anchoring of nuclear power plants in the Constitution very vehemently. [[Abg. Dr. Fekter: Well, na, constitutional air fires don't want the ÖVP!]] [[Abg. Head: I don't have it! It's for reading!]] - Then I misunderstand you. Good. In principle, we would have nothing to do with such an anchoring in the Constitution, but in addition to the power plants, nuclear weapons, both their stationation and their transit and transport, would have to be included. I think that nuclear weapons are much more dangerous than nuclear power stations. With regard to the present nuclear liability law, I would like to say that Austria is actually alive, as we imagine internationally, such a right of liability. There are already six countries across Europe that do not want to use nuclear energy peacefully. We want the number of such countries to increase. Perhaps this right of liability in Austria, which is to be decided on as an example for the other European countries, can be an incentive for economic pressure to be generated, for investment in nuclear power plants to be stopped, and therefore the abolition of the use of nuclear energy in Europe. Thank you.
ParlaMint-AT_1998-10-07-020-XX-NRSITZ-00141_d7e6026	The next speaker is Mr Magistrate. Barmüller. Please, honourable Member.
ParlaMint-AT_1998-10-07-020-XX-NRSITZ-00141_d7e6048	Mr President. Mrs. Federal Minister! Federal Minister! It is undisputed - and I can only add to what Mr Kier has already said - that the new Atomic Liability Act has a model character, and I hope it will also find imitation. In this respect, it would certainly be an uninteresting thing for any government negotiations in Germany to take this law as an example. It is true, ladies and gentlemen, that the central point of an exit from the peaceful use of nuclear power can only be about the rules of liability and the accounting of the real costs. This ensures that this technology does not have a long-term survival in a free market. On the resolution which is attached to the Nuclear Liability Act, which will be voted here today and which also supports the Liberals, I would like to make it clear that, if solutions are to be adopted through international funds, they will help to cover damage. This cannot and does not mean that there is a "healing" of national liability rules, but that is only an additional form of insurance and damage coverage until there are corresponding liability schemes in all countries. It is also intended to ensure that, if damage occurs, these can also be reimbursed. The fact that this is a different form of insurance already recognises that contributions to such a fund must be made. It is also possible to count some of the real costs on these technologies. Ladies and gentlemen! I would like to explain why the Liberals do not believe that there should be a junkification between the exit of the Eastern and Central European countries from the use of nuclear power and their accession to the EU. On the one hand, in the enlargement of the EU, we see a much larger political project, which cannot only be limited to the use of nuclear power. We are convinced that, although European integration must ultimately lead to a global exit from the use of nuclear power, when it comes to including the Eastern and Central European countries in the EU, it is more sensible to submit them to European regulations than to leave them alone with what they currently have and not allow for integration. We are convinced that the enlargement of the EU will strengthen competition, thus improving the economic and social situation in these countries. This will ensure that, in the event of increased environmental awareness and increased competition in the energy sector, it will be possible to ensure an exit from nuclear power use by taking real costs into account. However, we are also required in Austria not only to continue what we have now done in a pioneering role under the Atomic Liability Act, but also to take important and far-reaching steps in the field of environmental tax reform, because only under this condition is it ensured that the real costs are charged. Again: The central issue of the exit is and remains the liability regime to which nuclear power use is subject. The Austrian law to be decided today is an example of this, and we are glad that it is possible to decide it with the votes of all the political groups today. On the motion for a resolution tabled by the Greens, which bears the title "European nuclear exodus concepts and nuclear ceasefire": The Liberals believe that he is behind what we decided last July. It is true that a concentration of discussions on the future German government will be an important part, but to say that a new proposal, which only provides for bilateral talks, talks with other governments – and the first resolution refers to all governments within the EU and therefore also to the German – we think that we are too short. We therefore believe that it would not be useful to decide on this motion for a resolution and thus to concentrate on what we decided last July. Finally, ladies and gentlemen, I should like to refer to a request from the Liberals who wanted to achieve a change in the Nuclear Locking Act, a supplement to the Nuclear Locking Act. We not only consider it useful that the use of energy from nuclear fission plants is prohibited, but also that nuclear fusion plants should be subject to this ban. In this respect, it would be easy for Austria to define this in a law, because between 1994 and 1998 there were at least 1 254 million ECU spent on nuclear research, both for fusion research and nuclear safety, and just under 1 billion ECU for all nonnuclear energy research projects, including fossils. This means that there is still a serious imbalance between nuclear research and nuclear fusion research on the one hand and renewable energy on the other. And we think it would be consistent to exclude the option of nuclear power supply at national level not only with nuclear fission reactors but also with nuclear fusion reactors. We could thus provide an example at European level, which we will certainly be today with the adoption of the Atomic Liability Act. - Thank you.

ParlaMint-AT_1998-10-07-020-XX-NRSITZ-00141_d7e6220	Mr President. Mrs. Federal Minister! Federal Minister! High house! Today's debate and decision-making of resolutions or the Atomic Liability Act are another step towards a consistent anti-atomic policy that Austria has been pursuing for many years. The Austrian way is clear: With our popular vote against Zwentendorf and the subsequent formation of consciousness, Austria has taken a clear anti-atomic attitude. In recent years, we have also consistently communicated this attitude internationally and have repeatedly made our contribution to ensure that the security of the Austrian population is safeguarded and that its hazards are maintained by surrounding nuclear power plants. Internationally, that was not so obvious. I remember very carefully the debate in spring 1995 when I tried to find allies in the European Union as a new member of the European Union against the granting of EBRD loans for the construction of Mochovce. At the time, many European Union Environment Ministers said to me: Mochovce, Mochovce – what is it and where is it? They did not know at all what a danger to the Austrian population at the borders of Austria would be. At the time, we were able to take the relevant decisions against them: both here in Parliament, which has adopted a corresponding resolution, and also in the European Parliament, as well as in the Council of the Environment Ministers, which I am very proud of, because a majority in the Environment Council of Ministers was elected by an initial minority vote. At that time, seven countries of the European Union joined the Austrian position, and we had a majority against granting a loan for the construction of the nuclear power plant in Mochovce. Finally, it could also be prevented and thus a start of the EBRD in the promotion of nuclear policy. That was the central point: This loan would have represented the first support of a nuclear power plant by the EBRD. In the meantime, cost awareness has also become stronger and one knows that the nuclear current is not the cheapest but the most expensive current. It is not only the nuclear safety measures that cost money, of course also production, and ultimately the end-to-end storage is still not included in the cost reality. If these costs are involved, then the atomic current is by far the most expensive current. However, we have also contributed in recent years to changing the awareness of the European Union, and it is therefore a very special concern that the issue of nuclear safety and the medium and long-term exit from nuclear energy in Central Europe and the European Union must be and must be an starting point for the European Union's negotiations on enlargement. I am therefore very, very glad that the current President-in-Office, our Foreign Minister, Vice-President Dr Schüssel, called for a definition of the safety standards for nuclear power plants at the beginning of the Presidency – a very essential condition in order to succeed in these negotiations. Only these are good enough for the Austrian and European population. However, these safety standards apply not only to the candidate countries but also, of course, to the Member States of the European Union. That is why they are opposed to implementing the definition of these safety standards and are still rejecting an appropriate directive. The Member States of the European Union must set a good example. I should like to remind you of Germany, which made a decision very quickly concerning the non-completion of Greifswald, a reactor from the former GDR, in which there was the same situation as with Mochovce. The German Federal Government has decided under Chancellor Kohl not to complete this reactor because the cost-benefit analysis has shown that the cost of safe operation is not justified. There was a nuclear power plant in Bavaria, which was exactly a day on the grid and was then shut down and which will never go to the grid again. The Germans have so far been very critical of their own nuclear power plants. I think they have given us little support in relation to our neighbouring countries. He said in an interview that he would enter into an open and serious dialogue with Austria. We welcome this very much, and we think it is very good. ParlaMint-AT_1998-10-07-020-XX-NRSITZ-00141_d7e6484	Mr President. Mrs. Federal Minister! Federal Minister! Ladies and gentlemen! First of all, a few words on the four-party resolution request which has already been discussed several times. I really regret the fact that the Freedom Party has left the previously, I believe, very successful common path both inside - towards the Federal Government - and outside. There are certainly a number of reasons for this. However, I believe that the most important line to date would have been to keep it as it was. I must say to the ÖVP that I am very sorry that the ÖVP has been unable to separate itself from its attitude that nuclear power plants are basically evil, but nuclear weapons are good. If this had been done in their ranks, we would have the opportunity today to adopt a proper nuclear ban law in addition to the Atomic Liability Act. It is not so, and we have to live with it, but I say quite clearly here: I have no joy with this attitude, and I believe that this attitude does not increase the credibility of foreign countries. Ladies and gentlemen! The decision on the exit from the use of nuclear energy in Sweden and the emerging trend in Germany, I believe, is a huge step closer to the actual exit from nuclear energy at least in the European Union. In my contribution to the debate, I would therefore like to take a closer look at the energy aspects of these changes for us and the European Union. I would like to emphasise our objectives regarding the exit from the use of nuclear energy, which is still unchanged and which are as follows: First: At least in the short term, Western European safety standards must be sought. The behaviour in the European Commission and the comments today often suggest that this will be a hard work at EU level, in relation to all nuclear power stations, both in Central and Eastern Europe. Secondly, We are in favour of a pan-European exit from nuclear power in the medium and longer term. This one is to be pursued more intensively. Ladies and gentlemen! Many, I think, do not know that at present a third of the electricity needs in the European Union are covered by nuclear power. It is therefore, I believe, more than plausible that an exit from the use of nuclear energy alone should not be implemented from today to tomorrow. But I am of the opinion – and the development proves it – that an exit is nevertheless feasible. Energy efficiency should be increased accordingly. If I had more speaking time, I could now report to you on a symposia of an Austrian university on energy efficiency. This study shows, for example, that 400 million schillings are spent annually on heating and electricity. If more efficient, around 60 million Schilling could be saved – the first project results. So about 15 percent of the energy costs could be saved. There are a number of possibilities in households and industry to save energy. I do not think we will be concerned that in the short or medium term the lack of electricity from nuclear power will be replaced by electricity from gas power plants. Ladies and gentlemen! The reason for this: There's enough gas. The gas supply is already comprehensive. The efficiency of the new gas turbines is over 90 percent. We also need to know that the European Union imports almost half its energy needs, especially from the Middle East. The European Union's dependence on its energy needs is therefore a very large one, and the trend is still on the rise. If nothing is done in this area, then it can be assumed that in 2020 about two thirds of the European Union's energy needs will be covered by energy imports if they develop as much as they can currently be estimated. This makes the EU very dependent and does not contribute to crisis-proof energy supply. Therefore, the demand in the White Paper on renewable energy can only be underlined, according to which the share of renewable energy sources should be increased to 12 percent by 2010. It would be really nice if we could face up to what we have achieved in Austria so far. Unfortunately, this is not possible today. In conclusion, with all the measures that we have already mentioned and which we will continue to pursue in the future, we have set a course to implement part of the national climate programme and to reduce energy consumption accordingly in our countries. I think we should use this opportunity as a driving force for a new qualitative employment offensive. There are a number of examples of this, particularly in the field of the use of renewable energy sources. Nor do I want to know the hydropower. Thank you.

ParlaMint-AT_1998-10-07-020-XX-NRSITZ-00141_d7e6784	Excuse me, Mrs. Deputies. Mr President. Ladies and gentlemen! I would like to interrupt the debates from the plenary by means of a contribution from the Government Bank and to say a few words about the Atomic Liability Act from the point of view of the judiciary, in whose jurisdiction it belongs and by which both the assessment draft and the government template have been prepared. For us, there was no doubt that the Nuclear Liability Act 1964, despite the removal of certain gross defects by the Extended Limits Novel 1997 for many reasons, is unsatisfying. Above all, the objective of the law at that time, namely the promotion of the nuclear economy through numerous civil liability restrictions, cannot be maintained in the light of Austria's opposition to the use of nuclear energy. In a country that has turned away from nuclear energy decades ago and in which nuclear power is neither socially nor politically accepted, a regulation does not make sense which requires this acceptance. Even if one has in mind the generally cross-border dimensions of nuclear accidents, it is only logical from the point of view of Austrian anti-atomic policy if the liability rights of the nuclear economy are abolished. [[President Dr. Brauneder shall take over the chairmanship.]] The key elements of the draft have already been mentioned: drastic intensification of liability for nuclear installations and transports, elimination of maximum liability limits, liability not only for disturbances, but also for normal operation, liability also for environmental impairments, if, however, they, which will be the rule, are associated with a damage to a physical matter, liability for preventive measures – think of evacuations of large-scale nature –, facilitation of the proof of causality and the information rights of the injured, and aggravation of insurance obligations. But above all, as has rightly been said, the very core point of the new regulation, from which much is consistently derived, is – and this is, as has rightly been said, the current canalization of liability, which is based on international agreements. The cross-border dimension of nuclear accidents also takes account of our proposal through specific rules on jurisdiction and applicable law. In the future, Austrian victims may therefore choose whether to assert their claims before an Austrian court or before a foreign court. Thus, the choice between Austrian law and a more favourable foreign law is open to them. Although the enforcement of Austrian judgments may present difficulties internationally, I see no reason to ignore the material claims of the injured at all. All in all, I believe that the draft provides for a fundamental change in the field of nuclear liability. It takes into account the inevitable risks of nuclear energy generation, but it puts the affected and the damaged environment in the foreground of the scheme, unlike the current nuclear liability law and also different from the international conventions. It thus contributes to a remarkable re-weighting in damages law, to a reorientation that other countries as well as the State community can serve as an example and a model. Especially because of the cross-border dimensions of nuclear accidents, we have certainly not made it easy with the reform project. The explanations of the Government template give a debated and criticised testimony to the fact that all for and against the reform have been thoroughly assessed. If the National Council is aware of all of these arguments for the renewal of the nuclear liability law within the meaning of the Government's proposal, then this is primarily a political signal; a signal which, as we hope, will also fall on fertile ground on an international level and there provide an incentive for a rethinking in nuclear liability law. For the effect of this signal, it seems not least essential that the National Council adopt the new Atomic Liability Act, as already in the Committee on Justice, unanimously today. - Thank you many times.

For this very reason, because nuclear power plants are still projected in Germany, it would be wrong to concentrate only on Eastern Europe, and in this context it would be completely wrong to injunctify that with an EU membership. What we must do, however, is – I am finally referring to the Atomic Liability Act – to remind the legal requirement of the applicants. I think that this nuclear liability law, as we are going to decide today, is a major step forward, especially when we look at the international conventions. Whether it is the Parisian or the Vienna Convention of 1960 or 1963, it is totally unacceptable and clearly bear the manuscript of the IAEA, which it designed at the time. The fact that Austria is becoming a court place according to our law and that, in the event of a cross-border accident in Austria, the damage caused in Austria can be charged is progress. It will not be enough that only Austria has achieved this, but it is a good signal for the candidate countries. The motion for a resolution, adopted today by four parties, also mentions the need to implement the Lugano Agreement accordingly. For the candidate countries, it may be argued that if the Czech Republic or the Slovenian Republic wishes to join the EU, it is a condition to accept and ratify this agreement. In the event of possible dangers, a very clear compensation must be claimed. I am convinced that liability law can and will have a preventive effect. That's what you've seen in the United States. It is not for ecological reasons that one has gone out of nuclear power, but for hard economic reasons. If one finally ceases to subsidize nuclear power, just as it still works in a large way in Europe, when at last the real costs of nuclear power are taken into account, both in the field of compensation schemes with provisions and in the concrete price of electricity, then nuclear power is simply and simply no longer expected, then there will be an exit worldwide. The Austrian Atomic Liability Act will be a small contribution to this today, and I am very pleased to be able to agree with it. Thank you.

ParlaMint-AT_1998-10-07-020-XX-NRSITZ-00141_d7e7090	Mr President. Mrs. Federal Minister! Federal Minister! Ladies and gentlemen! In my short debate, I would like to focus on the government template for the new Atomic Liability Act 1999. I think it is, in several respects, a remarkable work that we have here, even though direct concern for Austria is perhaps less dramatic due to the non-existent nuclear power plants. However, it has certain effects that I would like to mention. In my opinion, the previous nuclear liability law from 1964 is completely obsolete. We all agree that today's technical, scientific, legal and environmental frameworks are quite different and have to be responded to. However, I believe that this House has already dealt with this problem sufficiently. Not for nothing there were two motions for resolutions on this – of 9. ==References== July 1997 – which has already worked on all of this. We now have a result, and I would like to address three short areas, which I believe are remarkable in the legal dimension of this legislative work. One area concerns § 6: security. Assuring, for example, for someone who operates a nuclear plant in Austria – this area of application will not be very extensive – security also for someone who wants to transport irradiated material, nuclear material through Austria. The guarantee is therefore remarkable because it is coupled with an insurance obligation in a remarkable amount: 5.6 billion schilling for someone who wants to run a nuclear plant. This is, of course, not the most important for possible damage, but a sum which also leads to an insurance benefit and a huge participation and premium. 560 million schilling for someone who wants to transport nuclear material! This means that this also builds a liability sum which could really lead to a cover of any financial claims. I also think this is remarkable in terms of an international role model. We all want to see a regulation in the international community that also improves this situation internationally. What is interesting to me from a legal point of view is the provisions of Section 11 and below. It is quite right to point out in the cause that – it is actually a new kind that we do not know in the context of compensation law – the causer does not have to be clearly demonstrated in such a way that the cause-effect principle must also be clearly demonstrated, but that the principle of probability is sufficient. This is interesting in terms of legal policy, because this means that one only has to prove that it is likely that the damage is due to it. If you look at the likelihood, you probably have to resort to the typology. In such cases, however, practical application examples cannot be found in the legal order, and it will be difficult to resort to this type of typology in the sense of this practice. Last comment: the question of a claim for damages under Austrian law, even if the injured person is not in Austria and possibly a foreign plant has caused a damage. This is certainly a positive matter for those concerned. The question of enforcement must be followed. Even with an Austrian judgment, that does not mean that you actually come to an achievement. We must also see, I believe, that this law should have a model effect, but that it should not fail to make international efforts to reach an international convention that really regulates such damage. This would also be the more important way for those affected in Austria and in the sense of the chances of success of the process. - Thank you.

So that you can imagine something below, I can read some of these decrees: ==References== May 1945: "The assets of the State unreliable persons in the territory of the Czechoslovak Republic shall be placed under national administration in accordance with the further provisions of this Decree." And so that you know what it means: "As stately unreliable persons: Persons of German or Madjar nationality." ==References== June 1945 – everything after the end of the war –: "With immediate effectiveness and indemnity, for the purpose of soil reform, the agricultural assets owned by all persons of German and Madjar nationality are expropriated without regard to nationality." ==References== August 1945: "The Czechoslovak citizens of German or Madjar nationality lose Czechoslovak citizenship with the day when this decree enters into force." ==References== October 1945: "The immovable and movable assets are confiscated without compensation, including property rights such as claims, securities, deposits, intangible rights of physical persons of German or Madjarian nationality." The law of the 8th century was particularly impressive. May 1946 – exactly one year after the end of the war – that standards: "An action that occurred in the time of 30. September 1938 to 28. October 1945", i.e. until half a year after the end of the war, "and the purpose of which was to contribute to the fight for the recovery of the freedom of the Czechs and Slovaks, or to the goal of a fair retribution for actions by the Occupants or their helpers" – one has not had to meet, it has already achieved the goal – "is not illegal, even if otherwise it would have been punishable according to the applicable rules." . We know what has gone ahead of the horrendous crimes committed by all of us, ladies and gentlemen. These Beneš decrees are valid legal assets in the Czech Republic. They shall be applied by courts. Top representatives of the Czech State confess to these rules and emphasize that it is not possible to get them out of the world. I can read something from it again: "With the day of the entry into force of this Decision, the ownership of the State shall be the property of all the assets of persons belonging to German nationality." Next AVNOJ provision: "A confiscation of assets is the compulsory, indemnification-free removal of the entire property, which is personal property or the personal share of common assets of other persons, in favour of the state", concretely by persons of German nationality. What about the rights of minorities in the territory of Slovenia? – The Slovenian Constitution originally provided that Italians, Hungary, the Old Austrian German tongue and, unfortunately, only in weakened form, the Roma and Sinti were recognized and equipped with appropriate rights. The Italians, Hungary and the Roma and Sinti remain. The Altösterreicher German tongue, whose existence has now been established in an opinion of the renowned university professor Karner – an opinion that has been drawn up on behalf of the Austrian Federal Government – do not have any rights, are also not recognised accordingly. So, as in the present Czech Republic, in Slovenia, legal property which does not meet the requirements of human rights in any way: here the Beneš decrees, there the AVNOJ provisions; and a deal with minorities and their rights, which also does not meet the requirements of the European Union to candidate countries, ladies and gentlemen! [[Congratulations on the liberals.]] We are now in the process of getting serious with the accession negotiations. Human rights problems, protection and recognition of minorities, the establishment of minorities with ethnic groups rights are never bilateral problems! These problems concern the whole Community, in particular the European Community and every state and every citizen within that Community. [[Congratulations on the liberals.]] For the right is indivisible, rights are indivisible, and human rights are indivisible. [[New condolences with the liberals.]] You can't pick out a part and say: That is nothing to do with the rest of Europe! This is exclusively the case of the Austrians. We do not take this as important as one or the other might wish. We already care about the people themselves. – Mr Foreign Minister! I can't! The problem of the violation of human rights in the Czech Republic and Slovenia, the disregard for the rights of minorities in these countries are not bilateral problems! These are problems that concern us all in Europe and, in addition, as I claim, all! [[Congratulations on the liberals.]] It is necessary to imagine what this would mean for the Union and for its citizens, in the event that both countries should continue to be members of the European Union, while maintaining these rules of human rights: Firstly, the existence of these rules would be contrary to the prohibition of discrimination because they would mean that other citizens of the European Union would be subject to severe discrimination in these two countries, not through historical reminiscences, only because of their nationality and linguisticity. Ladies and gentlemen! I cannot imagine that this would be in the sense of the inventors or fathers, in the sense of the peoples and in the sense of the representatives of the peoples of the European Union! [[Congratulations on the liberals.]] In addition, the principle of free movement within the European Union, which is known to be one of the pillars of the Community, would be seriously threatened if everyone who is German-speaking and German-speaking, wherever he lives, in Liechtenstein, the Liechtensteinians were and are equally affected, in Austria, in Germany or in other countries, and intends to take a residence in the Czech Republic or in Slovenia, so that politicians or courts can find themselves explaining the rules, Beneš decrees and AVNOJ provisions quoted by me: I can't! Similarly, this principle would be seriously threatened if someone who has initially slipped through and settled there – which is allowed to him and must be allowed under the rules of the European Union – had to expect to turn around sometime and put him back out of the country. All these are developments that the European Union is unable to do and observe quietly, and Austria as a Presidency power in these days is called upon to perceive not only the interests of Austria, not only the Old Austrian German tongue, but the interests of the whole union with all its citizens in this direction! [[Congratulations on the liberals.]] It is our Austrians who, because of our historical commitment and because of the geographical circumstances, know exactly what it is about, who know it better than the representatives of other European states and members of the European Union. It is equally clear that the two countries mentioned do not meet these requirements. And Austria has the power to enforce, in accordance with the principle of unanimity, that human rights can take place in these two countries and that minorities and groups are properly cared for. We got it in our hands! [[Intrusion of the Abg. Dr. Partik-Pablé.]] Question 3: The European Commission considers that the rule of law in the Czech Republic and in Slovenia is in line with the Copenhagen criteria. With regard to the exact interpretation of the Avis formulas in this regard, I refer to my answer to question 1. Question 4: In principle, what I said about Question No 3 was what I said earlier. However, Article 6 of the Amsterdam Treaty, which has not yet entered into force, explicitly refers to the European Convention on Human Rights. The final legal assessment of whether the legislation in force in Slovenia and the Czech Republic is in line with European standards and the principles of the European Union would therefore be subject to the Court of Justice of the European Community, the ECJ, in Luxembourg, in the case of the definition of the legal path by affected citizens, also in individual cases, independent courts and, lastly, to the European Court of Human Rights in Strasbourg or, in the case of the accession of those countries, to the Court of Justice of the European Community, the ECJ. In connection with Question No 5, I refer to the Avis of the European Commission. On question 6: As the European Commission has stated in its opinion, the provisions of the international human rights agreements referred to in Article 10 of the Czech Constitution have priority over national provisions and are therefore directly applicable. This includes, last but not least, the framework convention of the Council of Europe on the protection of minorities for the Czech Republic. ==References====External links== Slovenia adopted this Council of Europe Convention on 25 June. ==References====External links== Slovenia and the Czech Republic now belong to this Framework Convention of the Council of Europe on the Protection of National Minorities as Contracting States. It must therefore be assumed that the two States are committed to the principles contained in this Treaty and comply with the obligations laid down in the Convention. Question 7: On the question of whether such problems should be dealt with bilaterally, I absolutely agree with Mr Ofner: There are issues – and human rights and the validity of international conventions are undoubtedly one that cannot be discussed bilaterally. That's perfectly clear. For, of course, the validity of human rights applies to all the signatorys of such conventions, and it is even universal in the area of the declaration of human rights. The crucial point, however, is how we actually deal with things. A key element in the accession process is the pre-accession strategy in the light of legal harmonisation. This was the moment to enter into the Europe Agreements, which were concluded with all the candidate countries from Central and Eastern Europe. There is with each of these countries a separate subcommittee of the Association Committee on approximation of laws. With regard to property compensation for the displaced persons from the former Czechoslovakia or Yugoslavia, I regularly use each of my bilateral contacts with the foreign ministers of the two countries to resolve the still open questions. ParlaMint-AT_1998-10-07-020-XX-NRSITZ-00141_d7e12511	Mr Stampler. — (DE) Mr President, ladies and gentlemen, I should like to speak on behalf of the Group of the European People's Party. 5 minutes of voluntary time limit. - Please.
ParlaMint-AT_1998-10-07-020-XX-NRSITZ-00141_d7e12533	Mr. Federal Minister! Mr President. My Dear Ones Ladies and gentlemen! Before responding to Mr Barmüller's request and to the motion for a resolution tabled by Mr Kopf and Mr Doppelmüller, I would like to say a few words about the history of the teaching profession of the recycling and disposal technician. The 1992 Federal Ministry of Economic Affairs regulation created a new teaching profession as a training attempt, namely the recycling and disposal technician. In the original draft, the teaching profession was structured in such a way that from the third year of teaching either the field of waste or the field of wastewater could be selected and trained. Finally, however, for the duration of this training experiment, a uniform teaching profession, which provides for the training of waste water and waste in a teaching profession, was enforced. I think we can agree on one thing: In principle, the creation of a new job, which meets the modern requirements and wishes of the economy and of the workers, taking into account current environmental and waste management problems, is to be welcomed. Finally, it was an understandable wish of the economy – and I would like to say, too, that I see this – to separate this teaching profession, as originally planned, in a teaching profession of waste management and recycling specialist/waste and a waste disposal and recycling specialist/waste water. It was also a concern that it should be possible to use the graduates of this course of teaching on waste also as the head or deputy head of the entrance control of a landfill, but only for building waste masses or land excavation. Ladies and gentlemen! The regulation of April 1998 split the profession of recycling and disposal technician into two sectors, namely waste and waste water. This makes it possible to respond much better to the requirements and problems in the individual sectors. In order to be allowed to use recycling and disposal technicians as Head or Deputy Head of Input Control at landfills, the following shall be noted: Depository is not a landfill. Here we have to distinguish. There are different, depending on which eluate classes of garbage the landfills are intended for. And since the new regulation, the use of the above-mentioned experts as the head of the entrance control for building slabs and floor excavations has not been opposed. This is also the case in the profession. Another step in the wastewater sector is now also recognition as a waste water treatment plant. That actually makes everything okay for these two professions. However, on the requirement to apply the scheme to all landfills, the following must be said: In the case of waste and residual waste, the head of the input control shall have full responsibility for the proper implementation of the controls. Due to the diversity of waste and the removal of samples to be carried out, and much more, extensive chemical training is essential. For reasons of safety, I therefore take the view - and I believe that it is also shared by the Minister - that the possibility of using waste disposal and recycling technicians as head of input control at waste dumps would be too far-reaching because it is also our responsibility to ensure that nothing can happen. I believe that this is a reasonable solution, and it must be said that land handling and building wastes are the greater number of landfills and therefore already a massive upgrading of this teaching profession. The joint motion for a resolution by the government parties is only a final consequence of what has just been said. We would like to see the development of this profession, the number of apprentices and so on, and to be reported to Parliament after three years. Then you can see what has proved to be true and what to change. Political rapid shots are, I do not think, the right solution.

This seems too far-reaching to us, we do not think it makes sense. I would also like to point out, as it has been said, that this training scheme is too narrow, that it is very likely that there is a possibility of changing to related teaching professions such as that of the chemical laboratory. It can be obtained through a simplified additional examination of the qualification for the profession of chemical laboratory, and then, in the presence of a corresponding practice, a two-year practice, including the management of the input control of a residual and mass waste dump. With all due regard to Mr Barmüller's initiative, I believe that it was in the direction of the rejection of any type of deposit for graduates of this direction that Barmüller wanted to take. On the other hand, there should be and will be interesting employment opportunities for recycling and disposal professionals in the area of building waste and land handling sites, in particular as a waste agent in the economy. This amendment to the Rules of Procedure introduces some improvements, particularly as regards deadlines. So far, it has been planned that the preliminary consultation of a popular desire should take place within one month after allocation and that the National Council should at least receive a report after another six months. This six-month frist is now shortened to four months. Plenipotentiaries of a people's desire are given the right to take two more representatives, general debates and "hearings" – under quotation marks – are publicly held, sound and image images will be allowed to report the Committee's reports to a people's desire, to be sent to the Plenipotentiaries and their alternates, whose publication is in the "Vienna Newspaper", and in addition, the right to support any citizen who is entitled to support a popular desire, the reports when he requests them to be sent free of charge. It is not, ladies and gentlemen, just as small a change as you think, and I regret that the debate in the Committee has ended in the way that the opposition parties have, above all, been concerned to say what they would like to have on other things, and, compared to this huge awareness of wishes, what is now being decided is small. The fact that five Members can force a short debate on the Committee on Inquiry's proposals et cetera can force five Members to hold a debate on the declaration made by a member of the Federal Government is only a minority of the European countries. The same is true of the urgent treatment of a written request. The provision that the matter will be determined alternately in the current hour by the political groups naturally meets the smaller groups. The objections to the agenda are another example. ParlaMint-AT_1998-10-07-020-XX-NRSITZ-00141_d7e13095	Mr President. My very much appreciated Ladies and gentlemen! As the honourable Member has already presented the individual points, I can take a very short look and only add a few additions. The current own-initiative proposal for the revision of the Rules of Procedure has primarily met the wishes of the initiators of popular desires. [[Abg. Wabl: "Great"!]] The main content relates in particular to a faster treatment of popular desires in Parliament. These are essentially the following four points: Point one: In accordance with the current amendment, which has received more than 100 000 signatures, it is necessary to deal with them within the Committee within a month, and a report must be returned to Parliament no later than four months, which may also be shorter. This period is at least one third shorter than provided for in the previous Rules of Procedure. Point two: The Plenipotentiary of a People's Desire may now, if prevented or for other reasons or for support, nominate two other persons, usually alternates, who are entitled to participate in the negotiations in the Committee. Point three: In order to make the discussions on popular desires more transparent, general debates and hearings will be held in the Committee, provided that a finalisation takes place in the Committee, and audio and video recordings are also permitted. This has not yet been foreseen in our Rules of Procedure. Point four: The Committee's reports on popular desire, including minority reports, including those not yet planned, or divergent personal opinions, shall be submitted to the Plenipotentiary and his alternate. The President also has the publication of all reports of a popular desire in the "Amtsblatt zur Wiener Zeitung". Persons who are registered in the Electoral Evidence and who have their head office in Austria now have the right to receive reports free of charge on request. An amendment was also tabled to the Committee's own-initiative proposal, namely an amendment by Dr Kostelka, Dr. Khol and Dr Graf, which agreed on additional clarifications on the prevention of misunderstandings in the previous Rules of Procedure. Among other things, § 94(5) has been clarified. As to how this paragraph should be interpreted, there was some ambiguity in one of the last plenary sessions. It is now as follows: "In such a meeting, provided that a further meeting of the National Council is envisaged for the same day, short debates in accordance with § 57a and the treatment of a urgent request or a urgent request are not permitted." § 57a refers to written replies to written requests, requests for deadlines and applications for the establishment of an investigation committee, as well as to the treatment of a urgent request and a urgent request. They shall not be allowed to attend this meeting if a second session is held on the same day. The Committee has tabled an amendment by the Liberal Forum and the Greens which allows two political groups to ask for a committee of inquiry. A group in our Parliament can already be reached with five Members. That is to say, ten Members could demand committees of inquiry so to speak. I believe that there is really no other European Parliament! That is why we have rejected it.

ParlaMint-AT_1998-10-07-020-XX-NRSITZ-00141_d7e13960	Mr President. Federal Minister! High house! My venerations Ladies and gentlemen! The government paper on a federal law amending the Federal Law on the generally sworn judicial experts and interpreters primarily regulates the application, the conditions for it, the procedure, the Commission's activity and the listing of generally sworn and judicially certified experts or interpreters. However, these now have to undergo a quality assurance process – similar to a certification process – by a certification body accredited under the accreditation law. They are subject – and this is very essential – to their continued activity of permanent control by the respective court and the list-leading president. This special certification for expert or interpreting activities is such before the domestic courts, and the scope of the Accreditation Act is not covered. Ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen! I'm sure you'll ask yourself: Why this new regulation? – There are two reasons for this: On the one hand, there was the need in the economy or in the association of the generally sworn judicial experts of Austria that experts can also be certified. However, this new regulation has become necessary for another reason, and I see it as a priority. Let me say quite openly: This new regulation has therefore become necessary because the hearing law of the Chamber of Commerce changed de facto to the normative force of the facsimile in accordance with the law in force. That is to say, the opinion of the Chamber of Commerce or of the competent representatives of interests – I am thinking, for example, of the economic loyals – was usually met by the list-leading president, and there is no legal basis and no way of resisting it according to the old legal situation. My venerations Ladies and gentlemen! Allow me to state with all clarity that such competitive experts have been prevented. However, the new regulation is clearly improving the situation. An independent judge shall be chaired by a Commission which shall draw up an opinion on the applicant or the applicant and shall also carry out audits; the various examination steps shall be documented in detail. Two other qualified and independent experts are members of the Commission. This means that the application is transparent and can be understood by everyone – therefore also our basic consent to this government template. Federal Minister! However, despite this positive new regulation, I agree with some of the problems associated with the activity of generally sworn and certified experts. I know they do not fall into this matter of law, but should be regulated in other legislative bodies. On the one hand, the subject of the partiality of experts. Experts must have a particularly high level of expertise and experience, objectivity, independence and reliability, as well as sufficient knowledge of the nature and importance of judicial expert activity and relevant procedural provisions. I am concerned with the issue of independence, the subject of "consistency". In this context, we are faced with the problem of experts in their independence before or when ordering a court after the criminal or civil procedure – or if you want to: Imprisonment – do not be checked and controlled in detail. For example, it may happen that an expert is the majority shareholder of a company and by court order is responsible for examining the activity of another person, although he, the expert, is a minority shareholder at the company in which the person to be assessed is a majority shareholder. Ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen! I therefore believe that we must really discuss the question of partiality. We believe that the discussion we have held in the Committee on Justice should continue: that, before or after the appointment, at least before the start of the well-founded activity, possible grounds of partiality must be displayed or documented on a form. This should be clarified later on, because it is not only a question of asserting the partiality which can naturally be claimed in the proceedings at any stage of the procedure, but also of the consequences which you know: The consequence is the nullity of the proceedings. A second point, which I would like to address briefly, is, therefore, that the judges at the Landesgericht Salzburg have also asked me, that a legal arrangement should be established to ensure that judges can verify whether the opinion has been written accordingly after the court order, and that the payment of the fees can be made dependent on it. Another example is the award of orders. Sometimes judges wait months. I should like to draw your attention to one case: Criminal proceedings Non-profit WEB in Salzburg. According to my information stand, the judges had to have been on the expert's report for four years. I think we must ensure that expert opinions are delivered in good time. Let me also turn to the question of costs. Federal Minister! At the lawyers we have the so-called "cost shins". We will continue to discuss this issue intensively if we deal with the Law on the Law on Vocational Laws. Today I am concerned with the so-called time or line barbers of the experts. [[President Dr. Brauneder shall take over the chairmanship.]] It is not just a question of giving a thick expert opinion. If you look at such an opinion exactly, you can see how it is written: two-sided, or the sheet of paper is described only to one third. – Minister of State! What we need is not quantity, but more quality. Or another example – I quote from a Carinthian daily today –: Millions of disputes for advice. Klagenfurt tax advisor presents a 9 million schilling bill to the court. – I also remember the great WEB straf procedure: 80 million schilling of expert costs. If you know who did the job – that is not the experts, but the young academics who are hired under bad conditions – then you have to ask yourself: Why should the taxpayer pay for such high charges? Ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen! This law regulates access to the generally sworn and judicially certified experts with a list entry, while also ensuring quality. Federal Minister! This is primarily the responsibility of the Presidents of the Court: the President at the national courts, the Oberlandesgericht, in particular the Oberlandesgericht Wien. This applies in particular to charges. They all have their contribution to ensure that quality goes ahead of quantity. However, the task of this House and your ministry will be to continue monitoring developments in this area and to discuss the problems I have raised. ParlaMint-AT_1998-10-07-020-XX-NRSITZ-00141_d7e14410	President. — I call the Group of the European People's Party (Christian Democrats). - Please, Minister of State.
ParlaMint-AT_1998-10-07-020-XX-NRSITZ-00141_d7e14431	Mr President. Ladies and gentlemen, ladies and gentlemen! Today's decision on the novel on the Expert and Interpreter Law concerns a part of the judicial law, which is of increasing importance – and my predecessor has rightly emphasized this – for the entire judicial administration. In many cases, the decision-making process is only possible with the help of an expert. The expert is, as it were, not only a legal means of proof, but also an indispensable aid to the judge's decision-making in the proceedings. However, the activities of the judicial interpreters have also increased enormously in recent years due to the increasing international interdependence of social and economic relations as well as the opening up of national borders. The legislator must also take particular account of this changed role of judicial experts and interpreters in the field of justice. It is necessary to ensure that the most competent and best-qualified experts and interpreters are available for the service at judicial level. An important step has already been taken with the Novelle 1994 on the Law on Fees. In this context, we have also given the necessary equipment for remedying the main criticisms of the judiciary today. However, I will also take the suggestions made in today's debates to give rise to increased training opportunities. Furthermore, the review of the legal standards created at that time has also become a fixed part of the periodic revision programme. A further step in the aim of recruiting best-qualified experts in the courts is to ensure that this innovation is carried out, and I would like to point out two main innovations: On the one hand, the selection procedure applied to the listings for the inclusion of experts and interpreters is refined by extending the personal registration requirements and adding the registration procedure to an exact legal regime, with the addition of a technical commission. On the other hand, this amendment makes continuous monitoring of the quality of experts and interpreters more efficient by introducing a periodic review of registration requirements by the list-leading President. However, I would ask you to understand that we have to take account of the resources of the judicial administration in relation to the period of review that has been adopted, in order to address the amendment request, and of the measures concerning the already registered experts – at least there are about 10,000 judicially sworn experts. Finally, I would like to stress that the amendment also creates the necessary legal conditions for the conversion of expert and interpreter lists to computer. The aim is to provide the courts with a modern tool to ensure the rapid and efficient detection of the required expert or interpreter better than before, thus also contributing to the acceleration of the judicial procedures. This is another step in the efforts to make the best possible use of modern information technology for the court. – Thank you for your attention.

The signing of the agreement, which other EU countries signed some time ago, will now also include Austria in the exchange of extradition documents and accompanying documents by fax, i.e. almost with the most modern or almost most modern means. This acceleration is primarily carried out in the interest of the authorities so that they can complete the cases more quickly. This is also happening in the interests of all parties involved, because formal delays rarely have a positive impact on procedures. In times of increasing international networking, measures aimed at promoting and supporting the clarity and transparency of operations are urgently needed. The problem, which has repeatedly been complained of, that procedures which extend beyond the borders of a country can at least be reduced at the technical level. It is necessary and indispensable to define clearly defined control mechanisms which prevent the misuse of technical possibilities as far as possible. In the present case, this means in concrete terms that the agreement provides for the mandatory installation of coding devices to ensure confidentiality of transmission. These are, by the way, the only costs involved in this context. At the same time, a central authority responsible for the transmission and receipt of extradition requests must be designated. In Austria, this will be useful for the Federal Ministry of Justice. In the interests of all interested parties, that passage of the Agreement is provided for in the case of the challenge. If the documents are challenged by the requested State, its competent central authority may request the original documents or certified copies by diplomatic or other agreed means. ParlaMint-AT_1998-10-07-020-XX-NRSITZ-00141_d7e14855	We arrive at the 13th. Agenda item. A preliminary work had been suspended in accordance with Article 28a of the Rules of Procedure.
