ParlaMint-AT_1998-10-07-020-XX-NRSITZ-00141_d7e14882	Mr President. Federal Minister! Ladies and gentlemen! ==References== The meeting of the National Council, which took place in December 1996, discussed Austria's accession to the Schengen Implementing Convention in detail here in the House. Wide area included the fight against organised crime. It has been pointed out that in this context not only the safeguarding of the EU's external borders plays a central role, but also the so-called veiling of the fight against organised crime within the EU's borders. Fears that criminals could escape persecution and punishment by means of skillful deviation from one country to another, were conjured up by one and scattered again by the other. On one point, however, the parliamentarians here in the House agreed, namely that a criminal in the EU should not have the possibility of withdrawing from punishment. Criminals must be able to be prosecuted everywhere in the EU, and they must pay their fines appropriately once they have been sentenced. - So far, so good. What did not come up at the time of the debate, or what went down a little, are Articles 54 to 58 of the Schengen Convention, in which it is explicitly stated that no one may be punished twice for the same crime or for one and the same offence. What appears to be quite natural within a state territory is not so obvious between the states because of the various legal and legislative situations. This ban on double punishment, originally laid down in the Schengen Convention in 1985, was reaffirmed in 1987 in a separate agreement between the Member States of the European Union with almost identical rules. This agreement has not yet been ratified by Austria, and I therefore believe that it is a good gesture if we ratify it today, since we now have, among other things, the EU Presidency. [[Congratulations with the SPÖ and representatives of the ÖVP and the Liberal Forum.]] High house! I would like to talk briefly about one of the most essential legal principles of the Convention at all. This agreement, which is now before us as a government template and whose ratification is to be decided today, is based on the principle of law "nee bis in idem". This means that a person may not be sentenced and punished twice for the same crime. In my opinion, the ban on double punishment brings a little more legal certainty to the State community. This bill takes into account the fact that in the individual countries there are different dimensions of punishment for the same crime, and also the question of whether, in the event of a conviction, the penalty has already been executed, is being executed or can no longer be executed. In Article 2 of this Convention, Austria states that in certain cases relating to security or other essential Austrian interests, such as treason, espionage or the execution of war materials by the State territory, it is not bound by the prohibition of double punishment. It is very important to ensure the principle of "nee bis in idem" that those States which accede to the agreement establish a consultation mechanism between the competent authorities. My Dear Ones Ladies and gentlemen! I would ask you to agree to this proposal, especially since there is no change in Austrian legal understanding. This is a, if only small, but important and essential step towards a more uniform European legal area. Thank you.

This is a very important fact that we cannot stress enough, because I see this development as the beginning of a clear and good European People's Group Protection. The fact that countries are beginning to postulate changes in their own fields and, ultimately, also in the form of legislation – in the form of a Constitutional Law, as envisaged in France – to change to the positive, in the sense of the colorfulness of Europe, in the sense of cultural diversity, can only be welcomed, I believe. In this sense, however, it is also important to ask whether we have done enough in our house – now I mean the house of Austria, but I also mean this house in which we are currently in it – for a small part of our citizens, namely the members of the peoples. I think we are dealing with a fundamental problem which I have already underlined several times: There is a very rudimentary, very limited legal or constitutional protection of the ethnic groups. Again and again, the ethnic groups have tried – whether by means of the large draft basic national law, whether by means of a number of improvements related to the school law or also in other areas – to create a continuity of the protection of groups, regardless of the fact that people's problems are always political problems, electoral problems. The ethnic groups have no great interest in keeping up with elections – be it for one side or another – so to speak, as Waxman. In Carinthia, I can really sing a song of it, where the Slovenian question is raised again and again at election times, or the elections are given as a reason for not being able to do certain things. Therefore, an attempt to create a basis in a basic constitutional standard, which defines the rights of the ethnic groups, for the fact that, if simple legal solutions are not available, it is nevertheless possible, through constitutional courts or other decisions, to weave slowly the good carpet of solid popular protection. It is certainly also an offer from the peoples towards this house. This draft, which has now been presented to you as Article 19 of the Basic Law of the State, has not only grown in my club – it was important that the Liberal Forum has fully taken this up, I also thank the Green Group for allowing us to agree on a common paper so quickly, that was very important, that is the first basis, that is once the first basic idea for a good solution – but the Article 19 template is also in line with the groups of organisations, not with all, but it is the most important organizations with the party and would actually be pleased if we were to create this legal basis. It is not about bringing new complications into the Constitution, so to speak, but it is about drawing on an existing law that is written out of the monarchy and formulating an article in it specifically. So if, on the one hand, the European dimension we have already dealt with and highlighted today is so important, it is of course all the more important that we create a good basis in parallel with that within our country. As you know, the constitutional standards that regulate this, essentially contained in two international treaties: the State Treaty of Saint-Germain and the State Treaty of Vienna of 1955 – both of them after two terrible wars, both of which, of course, had to compensate in forced situations, emergency situations, lay the foundations for the existence of Austria. These are provisions which were not, in themselves, aimed at the protection of minorities, rather than a kind of compensatory agreement, which should somehow compensate for certain foreign or neighbouring interests. That is also, if you want to, the basic problem of these two constitutional standards: that the articles in question of the Saint-Germain State Treaty and Article 7 of the Vienna State Treaty have not been a priority from the need for the Austrians to do something for their groups, but that there have been political demands, demands in connection with the neighbouring countries, and one has somehow had to meet these demands and has tried to do something in the ethnic groups. That's the problem! That is to say, it is a waste of standards, created by a confusion of proposals – I would like to spare you, for example, to describe the creation of Article 7 – due to the great interests of the great powers of the time or today and due to the interests of the world. You know: Carinthia, cold war, karavan border, Tito, Communism – it all played in there. There were great new confrontations and the wish that Yugoslavia might be out of the big block and go its own line. So it was a concession policy. Therefore, Article 7 should be considered as a kind of concession article in relation to neighbouring relations and also to the global European solutions. [[Abg. Zweytick: That's dreaming! That's wishful thinking!]] I believe that it should somehow satisfy this House with concern that we are not able to create autonomous and authentic something new that comes from our own willingness to create a good people's group standard. I think that we Members should do something like that. We don't always have to let contracts go! We can suggest something reasonable ourselves! [[Congratulations at the Liberal Forum.]] I believe that it would also attract Republican Austria and simply fit us to close a huge gap in our constitution. We can do this in autonomy. We don't need to take care of anyone on any protective powers, on no major powers, and we can say: So we want to protect part of our very special, also Austrian, foreign-language culture! That is how we want to enable the people concerned to participate politically! This is a very important provision which I would like to point out. As you know, there is always a great deal of discussion on the principle issue in connection with the law of the ethnic groups: Is it enough to grant human rights? Is it enough to grant individual rights, or does one need something like Community protection, protection of the group? Allow me, as someone who has many years of experience in this field, to make the following very clear: In principle, we must guarantee both types of protection. Of course, we must ensure that human rights in individuality and for the individual are enforceable, feasible, indictable by courts, thus becoming accessible to individuals, but we must also clearly see that, if we want to preserve this Europe and also our Austria as a cultural community, we must also protect the individual groups as a community. We must also allow the groups to understand themselves as political, cultural and linguistic unity, because we only speak to others. I always allow myself to bring this into a somewhat blatant form. Since I am a Carinthian, you may also understand the example of singing: One must allow one to sing solo, to sing with someone in duets, but one must also allow people to sing in the choir. The choir, however, is not made up of many soloists, but the choir makes it clear that there is an interplay of the voices. Ladies and gentlemen! With this proposal, we have the opportunity to create a whole new protection of groups, especially taking into account subsidiarity. In principle, it must be assumed that people should manage themselves, decide for themselves – this is also a very important concern for the Liberal Forum – that they should also solve the problems they themselves are able to solve. Policy has the task of defining the framework for this. That is why the Austrian ethnic groups need self-government, including for their survival. But this requires clear constitutional protection, a collective one as well as an individual, and above all the necessary support is needed. I understand, by means of support, not only financial resources, which are, of course, necessary, but also, above all, in terms of promoting, that we can create the legal framework in such a way as to enable groups to reach their rights. I think that's important. In connection with ethnic groups, we are very often faced with the question of the legitimacy of representatives of the ethnic groups. I am referring to the very simple and clear position that this question is fully justified. In other words, I do not believe that anyone who stands up as a people's group representative should also be allowed to ask the question of legitimacy. At least with a large part of the ethnic groups, I take the view that we also have to come to legitimize in the area of elections. That is to say, it is necessary to create the conditions for an election. We must be afraid of the peoples to participate in elections so that they can elect their representatives so that we can have strong partners. We have thus reached back to the foundations of our democracy: We need partnership democracy up to the last village, to the far-reaching community. We must give the citizen the feeling that he is part of democracy. The groups in Austria also want to be part of this Austrian democracy! [[Congratulations on the Liberal Forum and on the Greens.]] In the context of this, we see as quite essential the provision contained in the last paragraph of our request. It is a matter of saying this with a term of technician, of the question of the right of association, that is to say, not only because of its own personal rights, but also for the group, but also because of the others. It will be the first step that will allow groups of organisations to take the right to take care of the concerns of the groups, but also of the concerns of the individual members of the groups. It is precisely this key power that one is legally able to enforce rights not only if one is directly concerned, but also if there is the possibility that an organization, an association may act and say: I will enforce these rights for us, for you! This is important for individual rights. But it is even more important for all those rights that the group as such needs to survive. Ladies and gentlemen! In this sense, I will be very pleased to see the discussion in the Committee. I suppose that you want to take this big step with me – from the Freedom Party to the ÖVP to the Social Democrats. The Greens are already involved in the joint request. I believe that we can take this step together. In this context, I would like to thank Mr Neisser in particular for having already put himself before the press with us when he first presented this request and also said: At least this is a fundamental right if we do not already produce the large fundamental right catalogue! I would also like to thank you for some very positive comments made by Mr Posch of the SPÖ, who has also said encouragingly. Please do not let the fearmakers and the fear-loving ones over and over again souffle, but let us take a courageous step! I feel responsible in this sense. I am now shoved in the mandate and I would like to try, in fact, to conclude these ten months in a reasonable manner. It would be a highlight of my humble political career, a beautiful, courageous, hard-to-hold article 19 of the basic state law on the general rights of citizens as a constitutional law. [[Congratulations on the Liberal Forum and on the Greens.]] In this sense, I ask you: Help me make a gift – not to me, but to the Austrian peoples!

ParlaMint-AT_1998-10-07-020-XX-NRSITZ-00141_d7e15362	Mr President. High house! I can agree with my previous speaker in many ways, in many ways. It would be more logical, in my view, to anchor it in Republican Austria, in the federal constitution of 1920 and 1929 respectively, and not in the basic state law of 1867, in other words in the state law of the Austro-Hungarian monarchy. I have no problem with many points of this request, although I believe it would be better not to anchor the details contained therein – from the regulations relating to kindergartens, from those relating to school education in the ethnic group language to those relating to the confession or the popular language in public life – in the Constitution. There the principles would be fixed, while the details – such as regarding kindergartens – which are not yet anchored would be contained in the respective federal or state laws. We propose, in the Constitution, Article 8 para. 2 of the Federal Constitution to include a State objective, which states that the Republic of Austria is advocating itself to its ethnic and cultural groups and the linguistic and cultural diversity resulting from their existence. I believe that this would be an important principle because the confession is a necessary one. I have also said this more often from this pult, I repeat it: I would also be pleased if it could still be realized in this legislative period. In this context, I would like to point out that there has been a number of improvements to the protection of the ethnic groups and the ethnic groups during the legislative period, starting with the Framework Convention on the Protection of National Minorities, which was ratified this year and with 1. It entered into force on February 1, 1998, up to the promotions created by the Private Radio Act, namely the possibility that the peoples – I am thinking of "Radio Korotan" and also "Radio Mora" – have to care for their culture, to care for their language. I think this is a very good thing. I would like to confirm once again, including from this point of view, that I wish to see the ratification of the European Charter of Regional and Minority Languages. I would very much welcome it if it were lengthened – I know that this is not the general wish of the Austrian peoples, but the majority of them – that a conference of the chairmen of the Groups would be held in Austria as it did last year. I believe that an institutionalized body as a contact person for groups of peoples would therefore be very important, because it would also allow peoples' issues to be considered differently from the different viewpoints of the groups, and also because peoples' issues are not free from party and power political interests and calculations. I therefore believe that these advisory committees should be strengthened and that this body should have been strengthened. Finally, it should be said that the Federal Government's funding policy in recent years has also been a remarkable one. In the last decade, the population promotion has been increased from 4.5 to more than 50 million schilling – the bilingual schooling has not yet been included. So a lot has already happened in this context. I believe that we are on a good path. But as I said, We would rather have enshrined the guarantee of the fundamental rights of the ethnic groups in Article 8 of the Federal Constitutional Law of 1920, as amended in 1929, and not in the State Basic Law on the general rights of citizens. Thank you.
